#7282. Senior Manager, Business Operations at Clutch

Clutch is at a pivotal moment. We're growing significantly year over year, and we need more connective tissue to keep pace. We're hiring a Senior Manager, Business Operations to connect the commercial side of the house, so the teams that win, onboard, and retain our customers work from the same facts and toward the same goals. This role sits in Finance.

This is a people manager role. You'll lead our Revenue Operations (RevOps) team and act as an operating partner to senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, Customer Success, Implementation, People, and Finance. Together with the RevOps team, you own the shared language of the business (definitions, data, and processes) so commercial teams make decisions from one view of it.

When something important doesn't have an owner yet, it lands with you: a partnership strategy, a new commercial model, or pricing and margin guidance for leadership. This is a fast-moving startup. We're building toward a much larger company but we aren't one yet, and most of this role has no playbook. You'll define the process, build the tool, and hand it to the team. If you want established structure, this likely isn't the role.

You'll report to the Director of Business Operations and work directly with senior leaders across the company as a neutral partner to every function rather than an advocate for one. Your work will be visible, and it will significantly influence how we make commercial decisions.

You'll lead a small, high-ownership RevOps team that runs the systems and data our commercial teams depend on, and creates leverage for them to do their jobs even better. The team doesn't just own systems. They build automation, tooling, and processes, and they're close partners to Sales and Marketing. You'll coach them, set priorities, and open up more strategic scope, while giving them real autonomy.

Learn the business end to end: the revenue funnel, the customer lifecycle, how each commercial team works day to day, and where decisions get stuck

Build trust with your RevOps team: learn the work they own, and set priorities and team rhythms together

Deliver the first version of client-level margin and cost-to-serve, the analysis that grounds our pricing strategy

Develop a first point of view on pricing and packaging for revenue leadership, built on that analysis

Put AI to work in your own workflow from week one, and identify the first set of manual, repetitive processes across the commercial teams worth automating

Stand up a first version of a deal desk, including an approval workflow, pricing and discount guardrails, and standard commercial terms

Partner with Sales and Marketing leaders as they prepare for the monthly business review: pressure-test their plans and point out risks, then solve them with the RevOps team by creating new processes, tooling, or automation

Build on the strength of the RevOps team: prioritize its roadmap against business outcomes and take on more strategic work together

Launch your first AI-enabled workflows or automations that remove manual reporting or reconciliation for the commercial teams, with time saved tracked

Have a team whose output and standards hold, with the processes, tooling, and automation you've built running without you in the room

Have AI-enabled, self-serve tooling that commercial teams use day to day, with measurable time savings

Be the person leadership trusts to pick up the next important thing that doesn't have an owner yet

5-8 years of total experience that combines consulting or banking with time as an operator inside a company

Experience managing people: you've coached ICs, given direct feedback, made tough talent calls, and built team rhythms

Startup experience. You've worked in a fast-moving, ever-changing environment where structure wasn't already there, and you built it. This is not a role for someone whose experience is only at large, established enterprises.

Strong analytical and financial modeling skills, including forecasting. You're very comfortable building models in spreadsheets from scratch, and they hold up to scrutiny. You use AI to move faster and sharpen your work, and you have the foundational modeling knowledge to know when its output is wrong.

Data-first and self-sufficient. You open the dataset before you build the slide, you're always looking for more data to test an idea, and you're comfortable getting into raw data yourself. You use AI to analyze it faster and know enough to check its work.

Commercial acumen: you can read commercial terms, spot the problematic ones, and reason about pricing, discounting, margin, and deal structure

A clear communicator who can walk an executive through a messy problem and leave them with a decision, and who is just as effective working alongside an AE, a CSM, or an engineer

Comfort with ambiguity. When something isn't set up yet, your instinct is to build it, not to wait for it. You create structure, manage competing priorities, and pivot when things change.

Relentless follow-through: you close loops, and people stop checking on what you've said you'll do

A partner's temperament: you challenge constructively without taking sides, you surface problems early, and you resolve cross-team conflict without escalating it

Industry experience in fintech, financial services, lending, banking, or credit unions

Please note that this role may evolve as our business needs change, so we appreciate your flexibility and adaptability.

Remote Flexibility: Enjoy the freedom of remote work from anywhere, balancing life and career seamlessly.

Unforgettable Off-Sites: Twice a year, bond with colleagues in exciting destinations, fostering teamwork and fresh ideas.

Paid Time Off: Enjoy flexible PTO days yearly for relaxation and rejuvenation.

Stock Options: Joining us means having a stake in our success, so you'll receive stock options as part of your compensation package.

Home Office Setup: Create your ideal workspace with a dedicated budget for home office essentials.

Work Trip Budget: Grow personally and professionally with a budget for work-related trips and co-working.

Health Coverage: Prioritize your well-being with comprehensive health and dental insurance plans.

Clutch is a revolutionary vertical SaaS company, proudly backed by Andreessen Horowitz (A16z), aimed at revolutionizing the way Credit Unions engage and change the lives of their members. As a champion of financial well-being, we address the urgent need for affordable lending solutions in an era where the average American grapples with over $155,000 in household debt.  Unlike traditional financial institutions, Clutch develops software to turn Credit Unions into FinTech lenders and leverage their balance sheets to responsibly lend to over 130M Americans. Our mission extends beyond mere financial transactions; we strive to fundamentally enhance the way credit unions interact with their members. By integrating cutting-edge technologies and user-centric designs, we help credit unions provide seamless digital experiences that are on par with leading tech companies. This approach not only preserves but revitalizes the longstanding tradition of community and member-focused service inherent to credit unions.

Clutch is an equal opportunity employer. All applicants will be considered for employment without attention to race, color, religion, sex, sexual orientation, gender identity, national origin, veteran or disability status.

Our organization participates in E-Verify. Click here to learn about E-Verify.A Note About AI at ClutchWe love AI. We use it often and encourage our team to creatively and effectively leverage AI tools in their work. If you join Clutch, we hope you'll bring the same enthusiasm for exploring how AI can amplify impact, productivity, and innovation.That said, during the interview process, we want to hear your thoughts. Please approach interviews without the use of AI tools—our goal is to get to know how you think, solve problems, and communicate. Once you're in the seat, bring on the prompts!

US Tier I (SF, NYC, LA, Seattle): $152,000 - $180,000

#7275. Coder Technologies Inc.: Senior Platform Engineer

As a Senior Platform Engineer at Coder, you will own the infrastructure Coder's own engineers build on every day. Our internal environments run the same deployment path we publish for customers, so when something does not hold up at our scale, we find it before a customer does. We avoid building anything so specific to ourselves that a customer could not run the same thing.

Platform Engineering owns three areas. We run the internal environments our engineers develop in, the self-service environments our field teams use to demo Coder, and the release pipeline that publishes every Coder artifact. You will take ownership of components across these and stay accountable for how they behave in production after they ship. Our users are Coder’s engineers and field teams, and you’ll work with them to decide what to build and how we know it worked.

Own one or more components of our Kubernetes platform end to end, from the Terraform and Flux configuration to how they run in production.

Improve the platform's observability, so that metrics, logs and alerts land in one stack with a runbook behind every alert.

Keep our Kubernetes clusters current, including version and add-on upgrades.

Apply cost and performance efficiency to our infrastructure.

Work on workspace isolation and container runtimes.

Contribute to the release and publishing pipeline for coder/coder, including its workflows, signing and publish targets.

Design for least privilege, with short-lived credentials and no shared static keys.

Write an RFC when a change warrants one, and a pull request a reviewer can follow without decoding it.

Mentor one or two engineers on the components you know best.

Run pilots with named users, gather feedback, and measure adoption and time to a useful result (e.g. from request to an environment someone can actually demo on)

Own docs, onboarding and support for what you ship.

4+ years in infrastructure or platform engineering, with direct ownership of production systems other people depend on.

Strong technical depth across Linux, Kubernetes, containers and Infrastructure as Code, Terraform in particular.

Production experience with at least one major public cloud. Most of our infrastructure runs on AWS.

Hands-on experience operating Kubernetes clusters, including upgrades, add-ons, autoscaling and the failure modes that come with them.

Experience building and owning CI/CD pipelines, and treating them as production software.

A working understanding of IP networking and HTTP fundamentals.

Has changed or retired a platform feature based on how people used it, or can describe how they would decide to.

Good instincts for where cloud spend goes and what a change will cost.

Clear written communication. Most of this team's work is agreed in a document before it is built.

(Tacos? If you need an ice-breaker, ask how we say thanks by giving tacos!)

Prometheus, Grafana or OpenTelemetry, including alerts you defined and owned.

Sandboxed container runtimes such as Kata, gVisor or sysbox.

Release engineering, artifact signing or supply chain security.

Prior work on developer tooling or internal developer platforms.

You still carve out time to code, either at work or on passion projects.

Infrastructure: AWS, Terraform, Flux, Helm

Coder is the leading platform for AI development Infrastructure, enabling enterprises to securely run human and AI-driven development workflows in consistent, governed environments. Coder provides self-hosted, agent-ready workspaces that unify developer productivity and platform governance. With Coder, enterprises can confidently evolve from human-only development to AI-assisted and autonomous workflows, without sacrificing security, compliance, or performance. Learn more at coder.com.

We believe that the interview process should be transparent, consistent, and enjoyable. We value your time and hope to complete the interview process in two to four weeks, if schedules allow. Through your interviews, you will meet a mix of individual contributors, managers, and senior leaders.

As an AI company, Coder embraces the use of AI tools, and we want to be transparent about our expectations as you navigate our interview process.

Not permitted: Using AI assistance during conversational interviews.

Permitted: Using AI tooling for take-home assessments. Please flag where and to what extent it was used in your take-home. Your submission will not be penalized for using AI as long as it is done honestly.

We use AI tools to help manage our recruitment process efficiently and fairly. Specifically:

Ashby and Brainerr help us review inbound applications by surfacing candidates who best match the role requirements we've defined. This tool does not make hiring decisions; it helps our team prioritize which applications to review first.

Granola takes notes during our interview calls so our team can focus on the conversation with you.

All hiring decisions are made by humans. Our team reviews applications, conducts interviews, and makes final selections. AI tools assist us but never replace human judgment, and these practices are conducted in compliance with applicable data protection, AI governance, and labor laws. Your data is not used to train AI models.

In accordance with New York City Local Law 144, an independent bias audit has been conducted on "Automated Employment Decision Tools"; results are available for Ashby.

translate security concerns into engineering action

can clearly explain complex technical concepts to others

are a fast learner and thrive in ambiguity

#7262. Target’s New AI Assistant Is Just For Browsing, For Now

While retailers have been developing agents that can shop on behalf of customers, Target’s new shopping assistant is not that—yet.

In October, Target rolled out a beta version of its AI assistant, Ask Target, within its website and mobile app. The assistant finds items based on shopper preferences, shares product facts, compares items, summarizes reviews, and checks inventory availability.

“We really are taking a phased approach in terms of AI and the types of problems it’s going to solve for our guests,” Purvi Shah, svp of user experience at Target, told ADWEEK.

Ask Target is now available to all shoppers after initially testing the feature with Target employees.

Mitchell Parton is ADWEEK's senior commerce reporter, covering retail media as well as the rise of agentic and social commerce. Before joining ADWEEK in 2026, he was a reporter at Digiday Media's Modern Retail and before that wrote for several regional publications in Texas.

#7245. Technical Enablement Engineer at LiveKit

LiveKit is the default infrastructure layer for real-time AI — the platform that gives AI the ability to see, hear, and speak. From powering voice for millions of ChatGPT users to enabling drone teleoperation at Skydio and serving as the backbone for roughly 25% of 911 emergency calls in the U.S., LiveKit handles the real-time media layer that production AI applications depend on.

Over 300,000 developers and 5,000+ companies — from leading AI labs to Fortune 500 enterprises — run production workloads on LiveKit. We've raised $181M from investors including Index Ventures, Altimeter Capital, and Salesforce Ventures, and we're growing fast.

Our product is deeply technical, and we sell into a brand-new category: voice AI infrastructure. As we grow our Sales team, our AEs, SDRs, and presales engineers need to understand how LiveKit works, and why real-time voice AI is hard, well enough to hold their own in a customer conversation.

We're hiring a Technical Enablement Engineer to own that. You'll build our technical training program from scratch, so ramp time doesn't depend on pulling engineers off the roadmap. You'll sit on the Sales team, report to our CRO, and have a direct line to the engineers building the product.

This is a hands-on, builder-first enablement role. You'll see the impact of your work directly in how fast new sellers ramp and how confident they are in front of customers.

are known as the go-to person for tackling tough technical problems

can clearly explain complex technical concepts to people who aren't engineers

are a fast learner, frequently picking up new languages and tools

The best way to impress us is to build something with LiveKit and show us how you'd teach it to someone who has never touched it.

Audit what AEs, SDRs, and presales know today and where deals stall on technical gaps

Build the core curriculum: LiveKit fundamentals, voice AI architecture, agents, and the competitive landscape

Ship hands-on labs and sandboxes so sellers build something real, not just watch slides

#7240. Brandweek 2026: Consumer-First Still Beats AI-First ft. Laura Knebusch

AI can raise the ceiling on marketing, or it can expose every weak foundation underneath it.

Live from the Marketing Vanguard stage at Brandweek 2026, Jenny Rooney sits down with Laura Knebusch, senior vice president of CPG marketing and customer experience at Georgia-Pacific, to explore what modern brand building really requires when media, data, retail, and AI are all evolving at once.

Laura gets into why Georgia-Pacific spent 18 months consolidating data sources, and how that foundation now supports in-house capabilities and agentic AI.

She also discusses why consumer-first and AI-first marketing are not actually competing ideas.

This episode is part of a special vidcast series that was recorded live during Brandweek 2026 and presented in partnership with Tatari.

Laura Knebusch is Senior Vice President of CPG Marketing and Customer Experience at Georgia-Pacific, where her team supports consumer and B2B brands across insights, content, media, promotions, marketing technology and analytics.

She began her marketing career at Procter & Gamble after earning her MBA from Duke and later joined Georgia-Pacific, where she has spent 18 years across brand management, category leadership and marketing capability roles.

Today, her focus is on combining classic brand-building fundamentals with the data, technology and operating models needed for modern CPG marketing.

Private Label Is Waiting, So What About Your Brand? — Laura points out that consumers have been adjusting their behavior for years, not reacting to one sudden economic moment. In household essentials, private-label options are credible and easy to access. There’s a warning lying there for marketers: if the brand does not create a real reason to choose it, loyalty becomes much harder to defend.

100 Data Sources, One Source of Truth, Eighteen Months of Work — When Laura stepped into her current role, Georgia-Pacific needed to become more digitally capable. The first step was not another tool. It was fixing the data. Over 18 months, the team connected roughly 100 different sources, cleaned and harmonized them, and built one accessible system that now powers multiple in-house capabilities.

Consumer-First vs. AI-First Is the Wrong Debate — At Brandweek 2026, Laura describes an internal discussion that paired behavioral science with generative-engine optimization. At first, the advice sounded different – Humans need simplicity, distinctiveness and repetition, while AI needs clear, consistent information. But the conclusion was the same: strong fundamentals work for both.

Why Georgia-Pacific Merged Shopper Marketing and National Media — Retail media has changed how consumers discover and buy, so Georgia-Pacific changed how it organizes around that reality. Laura explains why shopper marketing and national media were brought closer together, helping the team better understand where partners create strategic value and where investment should stay tactical.

Jenny Rooney is Chief Brand and Community Officer, leading strategy for the overall ADWEEK brand as well as the ways in which we serve and support our audiences with high-value content, products, partnerships and experiences, notably through our community programs such as Marketing Vanguard.

#7233. Week of Nov 16 Evening News Ratings: NBC Nightly News Is Only Newscast with Gains

NBC Nightly News with Tom Llamas finished the week of November 16 as the only broadcast with growth in both total viewers and the Adults 25-54 demo.

Nightly News’ performance was even more impressive in the prized demo, narrowing the viewership gap with ABC World News Tonight with David Muir to just 29,000 viewers.

Note: The weekly averages of ABC World News Tonight, CBS Evening News, and NBC Nightly News are based on four days (Monday-Thursday). Friday’s (11/20/2026) ABC News’ broadcast was retitled to WNT-ABC, CBS Evening News and NBC Nightly News were retitled to CBS Evening Nws, and NBC Nitely News, respectively. These broadcasts are excluded from the weekly and season averages.

According to Nielsen national live+same-day big data and program ratings, ABC World News Tonight averaged 9.09 million total viewers and 1.267 million Adults 25-54 demo viewers for the week of Nov. 16. The evening newscast was down 2% in total viewers and 7% in the A25-54 viewers compared to the previous week. Compared to the same week in 2025 (the week beginning Nov. 17), WNT was up 14% in total viewers and 27% in the demo.

NBC Nightly News took the No. 2 spot in total viewers and the demo, averaging 7.366 million and 1.238 million viewers, respectively. Compared to the previous week, Nightly News grew by 3% in total viewers and 7% in the demo. Looking at its performance to the same week a year ago, Nightly News was up by 14% in total viewers and 43% in the demo.

CBS Evening News closed out the week of Nov. 16 with 4.628 million total viewers and 650,000 demo viewers. CBS News’ newscast was flat in total viewers and down 6% in the demo compared to the previous week. Year-ago week numbers showed CBS Evening News up 12% in total viewers and 23% in the demo.

#7226. Want to Sell an Agency? Launch a Creator Marketing Firm

This story was originally published in On Background with Mark Stenberg, a free weekly newsletter that explores the key themes shaping the media industry. You can sign up for it here.

In recent months, the market for creator and social agencies has been, to put it lightly, brisk.

In September, the Madrid-based social agency Samy acquired Get Engaged, a U.S. social and influencer shop, in a deal valued in the low nine figures.

In October, Billion Dollar Boy Group bought Creator Authority, a business-to-business influencer agency, and the private equity firm Shamrock Capital, which owns ADWEEK, made an eight-figure investment in the social-first agency Saylor.

Earlier this month, the social-first agency LV8 merged with its sister company Mirage Digital on the back of a “high eight figures” investment from Velocity Capital Management. And last week, the private equity firm Falfurrias Management Partners took a majority stake in Freeman & Forrest, another B2B creator agency.

The deals are part of a record year, according to James Creech, founder of the creator M&A advisory firm Quartermast. There have been 30 agency acquisitions in the creator economy so far in 2026, up from 20 in all of 2025 and above the previous high of 28 in 2022, according to Quartermast data.

Overall, there have been 124 M&A creator-adjacent deals this year, the busiest on record, and roughly half of the agency deals have come since July, per Quartermast.

Behind the surge is a crop of boutiques founded during the pandemic-era influencer boom that have matured into businesses worth buying, which are run by founders who need more resources to keep up with what clients are asking for.

A specific set of firms—social-first creative shops, podcast agencies, TikTok Shop specialists, and B2B creator plays—are responsible for the majority of this interest, while the deals themselves are increasingly financed by a new cohort of buyers, including private equity firms, strategic roll-ups, and international firms. All of it is unfolding in a frothy market for creator economy deals, which is just now beginning to consolidate in response to urgent demand across a fragmented space.

“Creator marketing works,” Creech said. “Brands and their agency partners are going to continue to invest there.”

The current rush of creator agency activity has its origins in the pandemic, according to LV8 chief executive Griffin Haddrill.

Lockdown acted as a digital accelerant, pulling budgets toward creators at a moment when television and film had dried up and out-of-home advertising had stopped working.

Because acquisitions typically take nine to 12 months to come together, the resulting deal volume spiked in 2021 and 2022, slowed over the following two years, and has since normalized, Creech said.

The same period produced a wave of new agencies, many of which were founded by executives who saw the emerging relevance of the creator ecosystem and launched startups to service it. Five years later, those boutiques have matured into businesses worth buying, according to Haddrill.

FOR Scoops, Sharp Analysis, and Insider Context

For founders, selling offers the resources to keep up with clients, according to Get Engaged co-founder Ben Hiott.

“Social is becoming the center of how brands build their businesses, and clients are asking us to do more every year,” Hiott said in October.

The wave of dealmaking that followed the pandemic was primarily influencer agencies, according to Creech, while this year, the variety of targets has expanded.

In 2026, buyers have gone after social-first creative agencies, such as Get Engaged, OK Cool, and Sway Social; podcast and audio shops like Ad Results Media and Listening Party Agency; and TikTok Shop specialists including Orca, Uptickk, and Yada.

Private equity is also getting more involved in the space, investing capital after years of financing strategic acquirers, as Bridgepoint does with Samy, according to Creech. This year, Shamrock Capital took a majority stake in Saylor, Velocity became a near-equal partner in LV8, and Falfurrias bought Freeman & Forrest, among other deals.

Serial acquirers are consolidating the field as well. Influencer agency Ykone has made four acquisitions in 2026, and creator company network Residence has made two, while Samy and Publicis remain active, according to Creech. After large platform deals for Influential and Captiv8, Publicis has turned to regional acquisitions, including the French creator agency Point d’Orgue, to enter new markets, according to Creech.

For investors, the opportunity lies in stitching these shops together, according to Haddrill.

“The consumer demand was there, but the agency space was fragmented,” Haddrill said. “Now, investors are seeing this fragmentation and working to bring them together.”

B2B creator marketing has also emerged as its own category.

Creator Authority, whose clients include Amazon, Adobe, and Mastercard, was Billion Dollar Boy Group’s first acquisition, an all-cash, multimillion-dollar deal with an earnout component, according to Business Insider.

Freeman & Forrest, founded in 2024 by Emily Freeman and Forrest Brazeal after they ran influencer programs at Microsoft and Google Cloud, helps tech companies like Amazon and Google reach technical customers through creators.

And more of the deals cross borders: Get Engaged gave Samy a U.S. base after the agency built its business in Europe and Latin America, and dealmaking between the U.S. and the U.K. has picked up in both directions, according to Creech.

As the ecosystem matures, the next wave of dealmaking may come from the middle of the market, according to a creator management executive who asked not to be named.

The largest independents, such as Whalar and Influential, have already sold to Accenture Song and Publicis, respectively, which leaves room for midsize independents, many of which will come to market over the next 12 months, the executive said.

Creator management firms, which run creators’ businesses rather than simply booking their brand deals, are likely the next category to consolidate, the executive added.

The buying, in other words, is far from over, according to Creech.

“It’s mature, it’s scaled, it’s professionalized. The work is not done,” Creech said. “We’re maybe entering middle innings.”

Hearst CMO Heads Out (SCOOP): The chief marketing officer at Hearst Magazines, Sherri Chambers, will be leaving the organization in a few weeks, according to sources. Chambers, who joined the digital media firm in December 2024, confirmed the news, while a Hearst Magazines spokesperson expressed their gratitude for Chambers in a statement. The departure is the latest in a series of executive shake-ups. In July, Hearst Magazines appointed Chris Berend as its first chief content and experience officer, and in October, it named A+E executive Juliana Stock as its new president, replacing Debi Chirichella, who will become chair of the group. The legacy media firm has worked hard to cultivate a reputation for calm and consistency in an otherwise turbulent industry, but it certainly looks as if the company is aiming to turn some kind of corner.

Bunny Boss Bolts: The editor in chief of Playboy, Phillip Picardi, has left the organization after less than a year, Puck reported earlier this week. According to a company spokesperson, the move was a personal decision, with Picardi returning to Los Angeles after Playboy set up headquarters in—where else—Miami. But Picardi only began the job officially in March, meaning his tenure at the publisher only lasted a brief eight months. Given all the fanfare surrounding his appointment, I have to imagine something went seriously awry. Or Picardi, who did not return my request for comment, landed an otherwise irrefusable job elsewhere. This is disappointing because I liked the direction Playboy was heading under Picardi, reporting on the state of sex, dating, and relationships in this fraught moment—but maybe the loyal fanbase did not.

BuzzFeed Taps Influential (EXCLUSIVE): On Monday, BuzzFeed named its first chief revenue officer under its new ownership, tapping Chris Poydenis, co-founder of the creator marketing firm Influential, to lead its revenue division. The appointment comes as BuzzFeed attempts an ambitious shift in direction, positioning itself as a source of free, ad-supported television, bundled with the rest of the Allen Media Group portfolio. The decision to name a creator marketing executive as the chief sales lead clearly telegraphs the manner in which BuzzFeed would like to evolve, shifting focus to its creators and video franchise talent. Will it work? The odds are long.

Sara Kehaulani Goo is president of The Washington Post’s creator network, a new division that sits outside of the newsroom and makes sponsored short-form video series with independent creators. Goo, formerly the editor in chief of Axios, launched the first show in April, and the network has since released six series in categories ranging from personal finance to career advice.

Publishers increasingly want to work with creators, but the harder question is why creators would want to work with publishers. In our conversation, Goo and I discussed how The Post’s model works, why its creator content goes through Post fact-checking, and what a media company can offer talent who could go it alone.

Mark Stenberg: You specifically left Axios to head up this new creator division. Can you tell me about the role and how The Post is working with creators?

Sara Kehaulani Goo: I spent 12 years of my career at The Post as a reporter and editor, so it’s been nice to return in a very different role. The theory is simple: Look at where people spend their time, where they get information, where trust is growing, and where revenue is growing across media. The answer to all of those questions is creators. So, we are working with knowledge creators, people who have built big audiences around their expertise, and treating them as equal editorial and business partners.

Sara: We mostly work with creators who’ve built audiences with short-form video in lifestyle topics like personal finance, health and wellness, and career advice. Then, we develop the shows together and publish them together. Last week, we launched Her Next Move with an executive recruiter based in New York. She posts a two-minute video to her audience of a million people as a collab with us, so it reaches both audiences. We amplify it on The Post’s social accounts and publish it on our website and app. That show is sponsored by Uber.

Mark: Why would a creator want to work with the Post? Do they share in the revenue?

Sara: I won’t get into the details, but we pay them for their time. It’s similar to a contributor model, though not exactly like freelancing, because they’re entrepreneurs. A lot of them want to focus on the content, which is what journalists want too. We help build the business around them, and we provide distribution and editorial support when they need it.

Mark: Does their content go through Post fact-checking?

Sara: Yes, through my team. The standards are the same. There’s a lack of consistency in the creator world when it comes to disclosing sponsorships, and to quality in general, and we can bring that. Trust in creators is growing, but people still wonder whether they should trust what they’re watching on Instagram and who paid for it. We can help answer that.

Mark: It’s early, but how big is this now, and where is it headed?

Sara: This year has been a proof of concept. We’ve done six shows with six creators, across health and wellness, personal finance, travel, career advice, and entertainment. Now we’re deciding which ones to renew, and next year we want to do more always-on content. Brands are very interested, because every brand is thinking about its creator strategy, and finding the right creator takes a lot of effort. We take that headache off their shoulders.

Mark: Creators often relish operating on their own. How do you convince them to work with you?

Sara: Having led newsrooms, I’ve always seen my job as spotting talent, developing it, and creating an environment where people can do their best work. This is the same. Creators are entrepreneurs, and the platforms give them plenty of ways to make money on their own. But being an entrepreneur isn’t for everybody, at least not at every level. Media companies have always worked with outside experts. The structure just looks different now.

#7225. Senior Deliverability and Compliance Specialist at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. Most Kit creators don't have a dedicated Creator Growth Manager, and when their emails start landing in spam, they need someone who can find the cause quickly and explain it in plain language. We're looking for a Deliverability and Compliance Specialist to own deliverability support for that long tail of creators, run Kit's abuse desk, and make the compliance calls that protect the sending reputation every creator on Kit shares.

You'll also build the playbooks, training, and AI-assisted workflows that help Support and contractors handle more deliverability questions on their own, and you'll partner with our infrastructure engineers as we run more of our sending in-house. A creator can't build a lasting business if their emails don't arrive, so this work connects directly to our mission.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes digging through headers and bounce logs to find the real cause of a problem, can explain DMARC alignment to a first-time creator without jargon, and is comfortable making a firm call when a situation is gray. You don't wait for someone to tell you what needs fixing. You proactively spot opportunities, identify patterns, and take the initiative to improve deliverability before problems arise.

You'll report to Alyssa Dulin, Director of Creator Success, and collaborate closely with:

Our Deliverability team: a Deliverability Lead who supports our highest-value accounts and leads deliverability education, and a Deliverability Specialist who onboards and ramps up large senders moving to Kit

Our infrastructure engineers, who run Kit's sending infrastructure

Our compliance and anti-abuse engineers, who build the automated checks that keep spammers off Kit

Product Specialists and the Support team, who handle the front line and escalate deliverability issues to you

Customer Success Managers (we call them Creator Growth Managers at Kit), Sales and Migrations, and Product Managers

First Week: Get set up in Kit, our admin tools, and our support and ticketing systems, and meet our distributed team in Get To Know You chats (GTKYs). First Month: Shadow the Deliverability team and Product Specialists on deliverability tickets, account approvals, and abuse reports, then start answering queue tickets with review. Join the daily monitoring rotation (blocklist delisting, Postmaster and delivery monitoring) and the weekly Deliverability team meeting. Learn how account approvals, compliance flags, and our in-house sending platform work. By the end of the month, work deliverability tickets and abuse reports on your own and send your first delisting requests. Project: build a recurring report showing the trending deliverability ticket reasons so we understand what’s causing how to reduce friction for customers and how to solve it

Be the go-to contact for day-to-day mailbox provider issues with Microsoft, Gmail, Yahoo, Comcast, and regional providers, and write an incident runbook for blocklistings and provider throttling. Track the queue with a simple scorecard (volume, first response time, and escalation rate) and share what you learn with the team. Run training sessions for Support and ship your first process or AI improvements, like cleaner ticket categorization or better account data for our AI support agent, so more deliverability tickets get solved without escalation..

First Year: Deliverability support is faster and more consistent for every creator on Kit, including the ones who never talk to a CGM. Your playbooks, help center articles, and AI-assisted workflows are what Support, CGMs, and contractors reach for first, and fewer tickets need to come to you. You've led at least one cross-team fix with infrastructure for a recurring problem you spotted in the queue, such as an alert when a creator's domain authentication breaks or a check for sudden signup spikes. You're helping shape the internal tooling and alerting our in-house sending needs, and you take part in industry groups like M3AAWG.

Deep working knowledge of email authentication (SPF, DKIM, DMARC, alignment, and BIMI basics), plus the ability to read raw message headers and SMTP bounce and deferral codes

Hands-on use of Google Postmaster Tools and Microsoft SNDS/JMRP, and comfort working with major blocklists (Spamhaus, Abusix, Barracuda, Spamcop, and others) and their delisting processes

#7223. Outmarket AI: Head of Content

Outmarket AI · Remote (US) · Full-time · Reports to the SVP of Marketing

Insurance still runs on manual work. Most policies are sold through agents and brokers, and the people who know the work best are buried in the documents: checking policies line by line, comparing quotes, building proposals, on nights and weekends. We think that's backwards. The best people in insurance should spend their time advising clients.

Outmarket, the AI platform built for insurance, makes that possible. Account teams drop in their documents and get back finished work to review, with every finding linked to the page it came from. Outmarket connects to the systems they already use. The judgment stays with the person; the groundwork doesn't.

It's working. More than 10,000 people at over 300 agencies use Outmarket, including 30 of the top 100 U.S. insurance brokers. Most of those agencies found us through other customers. We raised a $34.5M Series B led by SignalFire four months after announcing our Series A because customers were pulling us forward faster than we planned.

Customers who want to talk. About 300 agencies use Outmarket, and most will go on record about it. Few content leaders start with that many stories ready to tell.

First in the seat. You'll be our first content hire, joining alongside a new Head of Product Marketing and Head of Growth. You'll decide what Outmarket sounds and looks like.

Real creative room, and a budget to use it. There's budget for specialist contractors such as editors, shooters and designers, and you'll have room to make work that doesn't look like typical B2B content.

A direct line. You'll work with the SVP of Marketing, the founders and customers, and you'll see what your work does for pipeline within weeks.

You own video, social, customer stories and written content. You'll build the function hands-on: you script, interview, direct and write yourself, and you work with a small group of specialist contractors instead of an in-house crew. Pipeline is the team's main number, so success means content that brings brokers into sales conversations, not just views.

Our buyers are owners, producers and operations leaders at commercial insurance agencies. It's a relationship-driven industry, and we're still learning where these buyers spend their attention online. Part of your job is finding out, then making the content that reaches them there.

Run the customer story program. Choose which customers to feature with our client services team, plan each story before the shoot, run the interview, and cut one day of filming into pieces for the website, ads, sales and events. Make it easy for busy customers to say yes, and never over-ask the same ones.

Ship every week. Build a steady publishing rhythm with a bench of specialist contractors. Find them, brief them clearly, know what things should cost, and review closely so quality holds as volume grows.

Turn our events into content. Work with our Director of Industry Events & Programs to plan what to capture at the conferences and customer dinners we already run, and publish soon after.

Find where brokers pay attention, whether that's LinkedIn, YouTube, industry media or events. Put our effort where the audience is, not where content teams usually start.

Build leaders' voices. Help our founders and other leaders post every week, each in their own voice.

Own written content, including the site, social and long-form pieces, and keep everything we publish clear and sharp.

Measure what your content does for pipeline and use that to decide what to make next.

5+ years in B2B content, brand or creative, including time owning a content program end to end.

You've personally made content that grew an audience or produced demos and pipeline, and you can explain the numbers and your part in them.

You're a hands-on maker. You've scripted, directed, interviewed and edited video and social content yourself, not only managed an agency or writers.

You've produced customer stories people actually watch, and you know how to run a great interview.

You've shipped on a small budget with contractors and can say what each piece cost.

High agency: you find the work instead of waiting for it.

Ownership: you own the outcome, not a task list.

Startup pace: we ship weekly instead of planning quarterly.

No forms. Just smart, optimized applications.

Remember every detail and next step, without a meeting bot.

#7221. Senior Analytics Engineer at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

Our Sales team needs a dedicated data partner, and creator commerce (how creators monetize by selling digital products) is becoming a bigger part of how Kit and our creators scale. Kit is growing, so we're looking for a Senior Analytics Engineer to own Kit's sales and commerce data from end to end.

Accurate sales data gives our team a clear picture of the pipeline and the confidence to make informed decisions. That’s what makes this role so important.

Your primary focus will be Sales: the dbt models that turn our CRM data into trusted pipeline, deal, and funnel metrics, and the Omni dashboards our sales leader and Senior Revenue Operations Manager use to run the business. You'll also model creator commerce data, so we understand what creators sell and earn through Kit, and you'll pick up reporting work with our Product team, such as feature usage reporting. On a small data team, we step outside our usual lanes when the need is there, so the mix will shift over time.

You'll build for two kinds of consumers: people and AI. Teammates increasingly answer their own questions by querying our semantic layer through Omni's AI and MCP connections to AI tools. That means your models, metric definitions, and documentation need to be clear and correct enough for an AI agent to use them without a data teammate in the loop.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes being close to the business, enjoys turning messy operational data into numbers people trust, and is happy to work on both the models and the dashboards that sit on top of them. When Sales and Product have clear, reliable data, we help more creators build more valuable businesses with Kit.

You'll report to Sam Umachi, Head of Data (Linkedin).

Sales and RevOps are your primary partners, who will rely on the models and reporting you own. You'll also partner with our Product team on reporting for features like Visual Automations and on how creators use Kit.

Within our data team, you'll work alongside a Staff Analytics Engineer who owns org-wide semantic layer governance and modeling standards, a Senior Data Engineer who owns ingestion and pipelines, and a Senior Business Analytics Partner focused on revenue opportunities. This role has no direct reports.

First Week: Work through our onboarding curriculum in Notion and meet the data team, our Senior Revenue Operations Manager, the sales team, and your product partners. Get set up in our core tools: dbt Cloud, GitHub, Amazon Redshift, Omni, HubSpot, Linear, Hightouch and Slack. Set up your AI-assisted development environment and learn the shared AI workflows our data team uses. Open and merge your first small pull request so you learn our review and deployment flow early.

First Month: Map how HubSpot objects (deals, pipelines and stages, companies, contacts, and owners) flow into our dbt models, and document the gaps. Take over day-to-day ownership of the sales models and Omni dashboards that teammates have been covering. Audit current sales reporting and list where definitions differ or numbers don't reconcile. Get familiar with how commerce data (products creators sell and the revenue they earn through Kit) lands in the warehouse today. Agree with RevOps and Sales on the most important questions they need answered. Review teammates' pull requests and contribute to our shared SQL, dbt, and documentation standards. Share a written reflection with the team on what you've learned and where you'd focus first.

First Six Months: Ship and maintain well-tested, documented dbt models for core sales metrics, such as pipeline, deal velocity, stage conversion, win rates, and the sales-led funnel. Expose those metrics through the Omni semantic layer, working within the team's governance standards. Rebuild or refresh the core sales dashboards so pipeline reviews and forecasting conversations run on numbers that don't need manual reconciliation. Resolve competing sales metric definitions by writing down the options and tradeoffs, aligning with Sales and RevOps, and documenting the outcome. Model creator-level commerce data, such as products sold, revenue earned through Kit, and conversion, in partnership with data engineering. Write semantic layer descriptions, metric definitions, and documentation that let Omni's AI and MCP-connected AI tools answer routine sales questions correctly, and test them against real questions from Sales, RevOps and Product. Get Sales to the point where routine questions are answered through self-serve, whether in Omni dashboards, Omni's AI, or AI tools connected over MCP, rather than one-off requests.

First Year: Be the recognized owner of Kit's sales data, the person Sales and RevOps go to first and trust without second-guessing. Give our revenue analysis work the sales and commerce models it depends on, so deeper questions about pipeline health and creator earnings can be answered quickly, thereby enabling us to make creators more successful. Leave your domains well documented and well tested, legible to new teammates, stakeholders, and the AI agents that query our semantic layer. Help raise the team's modeling quality through code review, pairing, and sharing what you learn.

Deep dbt experience. You build modular, tested, documented dbt projects, and you're comfortable with incremental models, snapshots, macros, CI, and scheduled jobs in dbt Cloud or similar.

Advanced SQL. You reason carefully about grain, joins, and window functions, and you write queries that are readable and performant on a cloud warehouse.

Data modeling for sales and GTM. You can model deals, pipeline stages and their history, ownership changes, and funnel conversion into clean, reusable structures. You are opinionated on Kimball standards and when a concept should be a fact or dimension.

CRM data fluency. You know how sales data behaves in a CRM, including stage changes, backdated edits, and custom properties. HubSpot is preferred, but other CRM experience (e.g. Salesforce) applies just as well. We care most that you recognize common CRM patterns.

AI-assisted development. You use AI code editors and agents (such as Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, or similar) every day, and you validate their output with tests, query results, and review before anything ships.

Clear written communication. You explain metric definitions, tradeoffs, and data caveats in plain language that Sales, RevOps, and Product partners understand.

BI and semantic layer experience. You can build dashboards and semantic models in Omni, Looker, or a similar BI tool, and you design metric definitions that people and AI tools can both query correctly.

Git-based workflows. You keep pull requests small and well described, and you give thoughtful code review.

Nice to have: Redshift performance tuning (sort and distribution keys), Python for scripting or API pulls, reverse ETL, and experience with product event, billing, payments, or ecommerce data.

Around 7 or more years in analytics engineering or data engineering where you personally shipped production data models.

Several years of hands-on work building and maintaining production dbt projects.

Working directly with sales teams and RevOps, and owning reporting they relied on to run the business.

Owning a data domain over time and evolving its models as the business and its questions changed.

Working at a SaaS company, ideally one with both a self-serve and a sales-led motion. Commerce, payments, or creator economy experience is a plus, not a requirement.

Working on a small or remote team where you were trusted to make decisions independently.

Ownership and follow-through. You treat your data domains as yours. You notice problems before stakeholders do, close the loop on requests, and finish what you start.

Judgment. You match rigor to the decision at hand, know when a quick answer is enough and when a request should become a durable model, and say so clearly.

Flexibility. You're glad to step outside your primary area when another team needs help, and you balance that work against your core commitments openly.

Teach everything you know. You document as you go, pair generously, and help stakeholders understand the data well enough to use it on their own.

Work in public. You share progress, open questions, and decisions in writing where the team can see them, including what didn't work.

Creator success is our success. You remember who's on the other end. A wrong number in a pipeline review means Sales spends time on the wrong creators, and that shapes how carefully you work.

Do it with excellence. You test your work, review it carefully, and don't ship a number you can't defend.

Candor with care. You're comfortable pushing back when a request won't answer the real question, and you do it in a way that builds trust.

Needing detailed specs or a highly structured environment before you can start.

Discomfort with ambiguity. Some of our sales and commerce definitions are still evolving, and you'll help settle them.

Needing several layers of approval to make modeling decisions within your domain.

Wanting to work only on dashboards, or only on pipelines, rather than across the transformation and BI layers.

Wanting a strictly fixed scope. Sales comes first, but you'll pick up Product or other work when the business needs it.

Wanting mostly greenfield work. Much of this role is improving sales models and dashboards that already exist.

Preferring to take tickets as written rather than partnering with stakeholders on what they need.

Being skeptical of AI's capabilities, or using AI without good judgment and accountability.

Expecting a large data team with dedicated specialists for every part of the stack.

#7217. 5 Black Friday Truths About Gen Z Shoppers and the Mall

Welcome to Black Friday—the start of the most critical five days of the year for retail brands.

According to Circana, 43% of Americans will do most of their holiday shopping between today and Monday, and nearly a quarter of them will do most of it today, full stop.

This is also the day that brands are focused on Gen Z, because 94% of them plan to shop this week, according to Deloitte, and they’ll spend an average of $752—more than any other age cohort.

It’s a given that many consumers aged 14-29 will spend that money online. But a lot of shopping is also going to happen at the place Gen X made famous: the mall.

You’ll find plenty of zoomers in malls today, in fact, but they’re not all spending the way you think, and some aren’t there to shop at all. ADWEEK waded through a stack of recent market studies and tapped the shoulders of a few experts to plumb Gen Z’s current relationship with the mall and what it means for Black Friday 2026.

Gen X might have been the original mall rats in the 1980s, but today’s 18-29 age group has picked up the mantle.

According to Coresight research released in April, 48% of Gen Z consumers say they’ve hit the mall in the last two weeks. A recent Ipsos survey found that shoppers aged 18-34 were twice as likely as those 55 and up to shop at the mall. And according to a Stagwell study released this year, Gen Z not only spends more time at the mall on a per-visit basis, they also spend $296 per visit on average, the most of any age group.

Gen Z mall affinity seems strongest with women, too. A 2026 study of female zoomers’ shopping activity conducted by snack maker Sunnie and Westfield Rise found that 86% of them praised the mall for giving them “energy they can’t get digitally.”

The reasons why Gen Z likes the mall have as much to do with what the mall is as what it’s not—a small screen. Mall shopping represents a rebound effect in retail form, a response to hours of isolated scrolling.

Coresight’s work states it plainly. “Gen Z has grown up with frictionless digital commerce as a baseline expectation,” researchers wrote, “yet precisely because of this ubiquity, they increasingly seek the tangible, social and experiential dimensions of physical retail.”

It’s worth noting that experts are also starting to see the same dynamic unfolding with Gen Alpha (aged 13 and under), whose spending power and influence is already approaching $100 billion, per Charles Schwab.

“A trip to the mall with friends can be an important first taste of autonomy,” Helenor Gilmour, head of marketing research firm Beano Brain, told ADWEEK. “That shared experience is a powerful part of the appeal of shopping in stores. It is why we expect younger shoppers to embrace shopping events like Black Friday as a reason to visit malls together.”

Brieane Olson, CEO of PacSun, is well positioned to observe the evolving shopping habits of Gen Z. The brand sells its apparel online, in freestanding stores, and at mall locations. It’s easy to assume that a generation that grew up with ecommerce would stick to what they know; or they might defect because they’re tired of it. But the truth lay in between.

“We’re continuing to see just how important physical retail is to young consumers, and there’s a real opportunity for malls heading into Black Friday and the holiday season,” Olson told ADWEEK. “Young people aren’t choosing between digital and physical experiences. They want both, and they expect brands to create meaningful connections across each.”

With over 520 stores across 4.2 million square feet and sales north of $650 million a year, Bloomington, Minnesota’s Mall of America (MOA) is the biggest mall in the continental U.S. According to its 2026 Holiday Shopping Survey, 92% of Gen Z respondents expressed an intent to show up there today, with over half saying they planned to spend once they arrive.

Just as significantly, MOA has charted a symbiotic relationship between the online culture Gen Z consumes and their behavior when they visit.

“Social media may introduce them to a brand, trend or community, but there’s still an appeal to experiencing those interests firsthand,” said a MOA spokesperson. “We’re seeing that with our leasing strategy. Brands such as Edikted and Pop Mart, along with concepts like Bandai Namco and Kpop Nara, reflect how interests that develop online are translating into physical retail experiences.”

“Gen Z may discover what’s next online,” MOA chief business development and marketing officer Jill Renslow added, “but they still want to experience it together in the real world.”

From a brand perspective, it’s not enough that Gen Z merely visits the mall—retailers still need to woo them once they get there.

According to the International Council of Shopping Centers, 60% of Gen Z mall visitors say they go “just to socialize or meet friends.” Sunnie and Westfield North’s work found that 72% of female zoomers would still visit the mall even if it meant they couldn’t buy anything.

But there’s still a marketing opportunity here. Coresight’s study determined that social-media content creation is a big reason for mall visits, calling them “aesthetically curated spaces [that] serve as backdrops for organic and shareable moments.”

The brands that benefit most, said the study, are those that boast “visually engaging environments with Instagrammable decor.”

Robert Klara is the senior editor of brands at Adweek, specializing in the evolution and impact of brands.

Select ADWEEK as one of your favorite sources and stay up to date on our latest content.

You can have 100 data sources, but there’s only one source of truth.

How connected campaigns transform retail media.

#7206. Show Your Work: Platforms Must Make Safety Decisions That Brands Can Verify

This column is adapted from the keynote address that X Head of Safety Michael O’Herlihy gave at the recent Brand and AI Safety Summit.

When I was at school in Ireland, math exams had a rule I thought was deeply unfair. You were allowed to just write down the answer—but if the answer was wrong, you got nothing. Zero.

However, if you showed your work, and your method was right but you’d slipped on one line, you still got partial credit.

It took me years to see the point. Showing your work doesn’t just give people an answer. It helps them understand how you got there. An answer on its own asks for trust; showing the work lets you check it.

I’ve spent two decades in the business of judgment calls: what stays up, what comes down, what is eligible for reach. Every one of those judgments is an answer. And for most of that time, our industry has handed people the answer and kept the work hidden. For too long, this has been a black box.

People ask me who makes better calls, humans or machines. Both make mistakes. If you give the same post to a hundred trained reviewers, you won’t get a hundred identical answers. So “human or machine?” is the wrong question. The right one is: Can you see the work?

Today, mostly, you can’t see inside. The systems that decide what you see, and what sits next to an ad, run out of sight. The deal was “trust us.” It held while volumes were manageable, and it stopped working as they grew.

Here’s the good news. AI doesn’t just make decisions faster. It gives us new ways to make those decisions explainable and testable. We now have the opportunity to turn the black box into a glass box: decisions you can trace back to the rules, and people outside who can check them, challenge them and help us improve them.

But a glass box alone doesn’t make a system safe. You could fill one with bad decisions. It would just let people see them. So transparency on its own isn’t the standard—defensibility is. Can this decision be justified under the rules? Transparency provides the evidence: Can someone see how we got there? Verification is the test: Can someone outside check whether the system consistently follows the rules?

Before returning to X, I worked at Reddit, where my colleague Rosa Català and I published a paper earlier this year called Escaping the Agreement Trap: Defensibility Signals for Evaluating Rule-Governed AI.

The usual test for a content-decision system is whether it agreed with a human. Same answer, pass. Different answer, fail. We looked at more than 193,000 real content decisions from Reddit and asked whether each one could be traced back to the written rules.

When the model and the human disagreed, the usual assumption is that the model got it wrong. But roughly four out of five of those model decisions could still be defended under the written rules. Disagreement doesn’t necessarily mean the model was wrong. Often, both opposing decisions were defensible. Grading by agreement is the wrong exam entirely: checking the answer, never the working.

How can two opposite decisions both be defensible? Ambiguity. Two reasonable people can look at the same case, reach opposite conclusions, and both be within the rules. Some ambiguity isn’t a bug, it’s what happens when real people apply rules to real life. But a lot of it can be managed, and it starts with more detailed rules. When we gave the model detailed written rules instead of just bullets, measured ambiguity dropped from 18% to 7%.

So how do we actually build the glass box? One window at a time.

Window one: See how it’s ranked. We open-sourced the For You algorithm, the system behind the feed: ranking, filtering and visibility-limiting labels. It’s on GitHub, and we keep it updated to match what’s running on X. Anyone can read it; you get the code, not a blog post about the code.

Window two: Add context. That’s Community Notes, where contributors can add context to any post. A note is only shown publicly if enough contributors from different points of view rate it helpful. The algorithm is open, and so is the data. Context comes from the community, not from us.

Window three: See what applies to your account. My colleague Keith Coleman’s team built Under the Hood. It shows account holders the visibility decisions that apply to them, and any restrictions applied due to a government request or court order. You don’t have to guess. You can look.

Window four: Understand the decision. This is what we’re building in Safety. Not just making decisions visible, but giving people clear ways to understand them, challenge them and help us get them right. When we label a post, the author will be able to tap the label and see what it was based on, in plain language, not code or model internals. They’ll see what it means for reach: who it won’t be recommended to, and who will see it behind a warning. And they’ll be able to appeal, telling us what we should have seen, so we can take another look and fix it if we got it wrong.

Sometimes people will still disagree, and that’s fine. Agreement was never the goal. What we want to hear is, “I still disagree, but I see why you got there.” A mistake you can see is a mistake you can fix. But there is a limit.

The glass box shows the rules and the decisions, not the answer key. We don’t publish the signals and thresholds we use to catch spam, scams and coordinated abuse. Open enough to verify. Closed enough that it can’t be gamed.

The past few years have tested advertiser confidence across the industry, and no platform, including X, is exempt from that scrutiny. That skepticism is legitimate, and the right response isn’t to ask for trust back. It’s to make trust earnable. Brands need to understand the decisions that shape where their ads appear, because what’s allowed on a platform and what you want next to your ad are two different standards.

The X platform philosophy of “Freedom of Speech, Not Reach” is built on that distinction. Lawful speech can stay up without being amplified. But limited reach should never mean hidden decisions.

Our rules decide what’s allowed; ranking decides what we recommend. Suitability decides what sits next to a brand, and that’s the brand’s call, not ours. Every brand draws that line differently; advertisers shouldn’t have to accept someone else’s definition of what’s suitable for them.

Control means setting your own standard. Transparency means seeing where your ads appear. Independent measurement means verifying whether it’s working. Published rules tell you what should happen. Transparency shows you what happened.

But neither, on its own, proves that a system is working consistently at scale. Verification does. Platforms shouldn’t be the only ones grading their own work, which is why at X we partner with DoubleVerify and Integral Ad Science.

To the platforms: Show your work. Write rules clear enough that decisions can be traced back to them, and let people outside your walls check the results.

To brands and agencies: Ask for it. Make “Can I check this?” a normal question, and ask every platform, including X: Can I set my own suitability standard? Can I see where my ads actually ran? Who outside your company checks it? Safety shouldn’t be something platforms ask you to trust. It should be something you can verify.

Those math exams had it right. Showing your work gives people the confidence to understand how you got there, and when you get it wrong, it gives them the evidence to say so.

“Trust us” belongs to the black box. It’s time to build the glass box. Show your work.

Michael O'Herlihy is Head of Safety at X, where he leads the work to keep people safe across the platform and its AI systems while protecting free expression and increasing transparency. He has more than two decades of senior leadership experience in technology, safety, product, and incident response, including roles at Google, Uber, Meta, Reddit, and X.

You'll own Postgres versions, upgrades and end-of-life. Which major and minor versions we support, how and when customers upgrade, and how we retire old versions. Your goal is to make upgrades routine rather than risky.

You'll make maintenance predictable. You'll design how we schedule, communicate and run maintenance windows so customers stay informed and in control, and disruption is rare and expected.

You'll talk to developers across the full spectrum, from indie developers on their first project to enterprises running critical workloads, and bring what you learn into every prioritization call.

Know Postgres well, or another relational database well enough to ramp quickly. You're comfortable reading EXPLAIN output and talking about replication, WAL, roles and extensions.

Have shipped products where reliability and trust are the experience. You treat downtime and breaking changes as product problems, not just operational ones.

Build with what you own. You run queries, test upgrades and try extensions yourself, and form opinions through first-hand use.

Use AI to move faster. You use AI tools to compress research, drafting and feedback synthesis so you spend more time on judgment calls.

Work async by default. Supabase is fully remote. You make decisions in writing and move work forward without meetings.

Worked on database observability, performance tooling, or security and compliance products.

How do we define Diversity, Equity, Inclusion, and Belonging at Talkspace?

Diversity encompasses the unique attributes of our employees as individuals. We value and embrace the richness arising from their varied backgrounds, perspectives, and experiences, which include, but are not limited to, age, ability, ethnicity, gender, race, and cultural background.

Equity refers to a fair and impartial workplace, aiming to ensure equal growth and advancement opportunities for all employees. This involves amplifying underrepresented voices, addressing unconscious biases, and providing inclusive, culturally competent mental health care.

Inclusion signifies the practice of granting equal access to opportunities and resources for all employees, particularly those who might otherwise be excluded or marginalized. It ensures that everyone feels a sense of belonging, value, support, and respect as an individual.

Belonging reflects the affinity and positive relationships that develop among employees from diverse backgrounds when businesses actively promote diversity, equity, and inclusion in the workplace.

If you want meaningful work, visible impact and put something genuinely rare on your CV, keep reading.

Hi there! We're hiring an Information Security Engineer with a focus on Application Security to join our Information Security team at Camunda.

We are a team that takes security seriously without making it feel like a locked door. We work in a transparent, straightforward way, and we care about doing the work right rather than just checking a compliance box.

You will work side by side with our R&D engineers: reviewing designs, threat modeling new features, digging into code and helping teams make good, risk-based decisions about the vulnerabilities that come in. You'll also help build the internal tools that make this work scale, including our CVE registry and the LLM-assisted workflows we use to analyse code and triage findings.

Camunda is an AI-first company, and InfoSec is no exception: AI agents are part of how we review code, assess vulnerabilities, and build our tools. You will help shape how that works.

This is a hands-on role for someone who already has solid AppSec foundations and wants to grow. We are a generalist team, so you will also take part in our on-call rotation and incident response, and get exposure to other areas of security. But most of your time will be spent with engineers and building and maintaining internal tools and processes.

Vulnerability management: triage findings from SCA, container and code scanning, external reports and internal security testing; assess whether Camunda is actually affected; agree fix / mitigate / accept with engineering and follow through to release.

Threat modeling and security reviews: run threat modeling sessions and design reviews with R&D teams for new features and architecture changes, and turn the results into concrete, prioritised actions.

Code analysis with LLMs: use and improve LLM-assisted tooling (agents, prompts, reusable skills) to review code, validate findings and reproduce issues, while applying your own judgement to know when the model is wrong.

Build internal security tooling: contribute to the CVE registry and related automation that connects scanners, engineering teams, and customer communication. Much of it is built on Camunda itself.

Help engineers do the secure thing by default: answer questions, review pull requests, improve guidelines and occasionally run a session or workshop.

On-call and incident response: share the InfoSec on-call rotation with the other engineers and help investigate and resolve security incidents.

Support the wider team: help write security advisories for our customers, answer customer security questions and support audits.

Around 3+ years in security or software engineering, with at least 2 of them working on application or product security (AppSec, product security, pentesting, or a security-focused developer role all count).

Experience at a SaaS or software product company, working closely with engineering teams that ship often through modern CI/CD and cloud environments.

Hands-on vulnerability triage: you've worked with tools like Snyk, Trivy, Semgrep, CodeQL or similar, can tell a real issue from a false positive, and can explain how exploitable something actually is in context (CVSS, exploitability, configuration).

Threat modeling and design review experience (e.g. STRIDE) on real systems, ideally web apps, APIs, or distributed services.

You read and write code. You're comfortable reading Java (Camunda is built on it) well enough to follow a data flow and spot a security bug. You can write scripts and small tools and contribute to an existing codebase with review.

You build with AI, not just chat with it. You use AI coding agents (Claude Code, Cursor, Copilot or similar) in your daily work to write tools, analyse code or automate tasks. And you know their output still needs a second look, especially when it comes to security.

Clear, friendly communication with engineers. You see yourself as an enabler, not a gatekeeper, and you can explain risk to people who aren't security specialists.

Some Python and/or JavaScript experience, or experience building internal tools.

Willingness to learn and use Camunda, since we build a lot of our own security automation on it. Prior BPMN / workflow experience is a plus.

Experience responding to security incidents or being part of an on-call rotation.

Exposure to security advisories, CVE disclosure or bug bounty programmes.

Knowledge of AI/LLM security risks (OWASP Top 10 for LLMs / agentic apps, prompt injection, tool abuse).

Experience securing Kubernetes or container-based workloads, or open-source / B2B software.

Worked in email, martech, or the creator economy

You lead without a formal title. You align sales, product, RevOps, and finance on goals, trade-offs, and what success looks like, then keep things moving.

Needing extensive approval before acting. You'll be trusted to make calls on speed versus quality and to explain your reasoning.

Discomfort with direct, candid feedback, especially from sellers on what's working in live deals.

We've raised more than $100 million from top VC firms like Forerunner, GV (Google Ventures), and True Ventures, with ambitions to create a category defining business assisting Americans in need.

Speaking with severely injured clients trying to rebuild their lives after a catastrophic accident

Sourcing and vetting local counsel in individual markets, holding a high bar for representation quality and ending relationships that fall below it

Working with our data and insights team to identify existing clients who may have unrecognized claims in adjacent fields of law, and setting the standard for which ancillary cases we take on

Building the playbook for claim types we haven't handled before, starting by screening cases yourself and eventually creating the protocol our intake specialists will run at scale

This is a rare opportunity for an attorney to join a profitable and growing public-interest law firm and take on one of our biggest priorities as a company.

You can understand the nuances of various contingency fields of law and can apply them to a variety of cases (including Social Security Disability, Workers’ Compensation, Personal Injury, and more) – you don’t need to be an expert today, but you should be a fast learner.

You have strong instincts and judgment as an operator – you can run effective and efficient processes, and you don’t let them run you.

You have a demonstrated commitment to doing good and helping people.

You’ve built a track record as a great people manager – you know how to motivate talent and hold a high bar for output.

You have meaningful work experience outside of serving as a lawyer, ideally at a startup or other fast-paced, agile environment (e.g., a business operations team).

How do we define Diversity, Equity, Inclusion, and Belonging at Talkspace?

Diversity encompasses the unique attributes of our employees as individuals. We value and embrace the richness arising from their varied backgrounds, perspectives, and experiences, which include, but are not limited to, age, ability, ethnicity, gender, race, and cultural background.

Equity refers to a fair and impartial workplace, aiming to ensure equal growth and advancement opportunities for all employees. This involves amplifying underrepresented voices, addressing unconscious biases, and providing inclusive, culturally competent mental health care.

Inclusion signifies the practice of granting equal access to opportunities and resources for all employees, particularly those who might otherwise be excluded or marginalized. It ensures that everyone feels a sense of belonging, value, support, and respect as an individual.

Belonging reflects the affinity and positive relationships that develop among employees from diverse backgrounds when businesses actively promote diversity, equity, and inclusion in the workplace.
